======================================================================
ARTICLE: Last mile (transportation)
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{Short description|Final leg of a journey in logistics}}
{{Use dmy dates|date=February 2015}}

[[File:Capital Bikeshare DC 2010 10 532.JPG|thumb|[[Bicycle sharing system]]s such as Washington, D.C.'s [[Capital Bikeshare]] can increase the practical distance within which passengers can reach destinations.]]
In [[supply chain management]] and [[transportation planning]], the '''last mile''' or '''last kilometer''' is the last leg of a journey that comprises the movement of [[passenger]]s and [[goods]] from their point of origin or a [[transportation hub]] to a final destination. The last mile in a journey could incur significant problems in a journey, even leading to people giving up on their journeys or using different modes of transport to overcome the final leg of a journey.<ref name=":3">{{Cite journal |date=2023-07-01 |title=Towards systematic measurement of travel problems: A pilot study in the greater Tel Aviv area |url=https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S2214367X2300042X |journal=Travel Behaviour and Society |language=en |volume=32 |doi=10.1016/j.tb |issn=2214-367X |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230611155811/https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S2214367X2300042X |archive-date=11 June 2023 |access-date=26 September 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> The concept of "last mile" was adopted from the [[telecommunications industry]], which faced difficulty connecting individual homes to the main telecommunications network. Similarly, in supply chain management, the last mile describes the [[logistical]] challenges at the last phase of transportation, getting people and packages from hubs to their final destinations.

Last-mile delivery is an increasingly studied field as the number of [[business-to-consumer]] (b2c) deliveries grows, especially from [[e-commerce]] companies in [[freight transport]]ation, and [[ride-sharing]] companies in [[personal transporter|personal transportation]]. Some challenges of last-mile delivery include minimizing cost, ensuring transparency, increasing efficiency, and improving infrastructure.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Khaled |first1=Naim |date=5 January 2023 |title=5 Practical Tips to Solve Your Last Mile Problem |url=https://onfleet.com/blog/last-mile-problem/ |accessdate=8 August 2023 |website=Onfleet}}</ref>

== The last mile problem ==
The last mile in a journey in the transportation of people refers to the last leg of a journey, usually from the moment a passenger descends from a mode of transportation and most traverse the final leg of the journey to reach the destination. According to John Pritchard and [[Karel Martens (transport planner)|Karel Martens]], in some instances because of transportation or land use planning the last part of a journey could incur significant problems in a journey, even leading to people giving up on their journeys or using different modes of transport to overcome the final leg of a journey.<ref name=":3" /> The last mile problem refers to last mile being the most expensive stage of the entire logistics journey. In fact, it accounts for 53% of total delivery costs. The factors for the high costs of last mile delivery are numerous:{{cn|date=September 2026}}

* Dense urban areas lead to more stops and navigation challenges.
* The surge in [[e-commerce]] increases small-scale delivery expenses.
* Customer expectations for rapid deliveries add pressure for costly express options.
* Maintaining a skilled delivery workforce.
* Rising fuel prices, vehicle maintenance.

The last mile problem is exacerbated in rural areas due to dispersed populations and lower demand.<ref name=":Liu">{{Cite book |last=Liu |first=Lizhi |title=From Click to Boom: The Political Economy of E-Commerce in China |publisher=[[Princeton University Press]] |year=2024 |isbn=9780691254104 |pages=128}}</ref>

The last mile problem is usually addressed by route optimization methods that lead to reduced mileage, fuel consumption and working hours. Businesses in the last mile sector can either optimize routes manually or use a delivery management technology platform.{{cn|date=September 2026}}

== History ==
{{See also|Last mile (telecommunications)}}

"Last mile" was originally used in the telecommunications industry to describe the difficulty of connecting end users' homes and businesses to the main telecommunication network. The last "mile" of cable or wire is only used by one customer. Therefore, the cost of installing and maintaining this infrastructure can only be amortized over one subscriber, compared to many customers in the main "trunks" of the network.

In [[supply chain management]] the last mile describes a similar problem for transporting either people or freight. In freight networks, parcels can be delivered to a central hub efficiently via ship, train or other means, but they must then be loaded into smaller vehicles for delivery to individual customers. In transportation networks, "last mile" describes the rising marginal cost of getting people from a transportation hub such as an airport or train station to their final destination.

In 2018, [[Amazon (company)|Amazon]] developed a comprehensive logistics network by employing thousands of last-mile delivery vehicles. This had notable effects on the logistics and retail industry, and introduced a new type of competition to both industries. [[FedEx]] CEO Fred Smith was quoted, "We basically compete in an ecosphere that's got five entities in it. There's [[United Parcel Service|UPS]], there's [[DHL]], there's the [[United States Postal Service|U.S. Postal Service]], and now, increasingly, there's Amazon."<ref>Logistics experts try to figure out how to take on Amazon. https://www.palmbeachdailynews.com/business/20200125/logistics-experts-try-to-figure-out-how-to-take-on-amazon {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20201128060116/https://www.palmbeachdailynews.com/business/20200125/logistics-experts-try-to-figure-out-how-to-take-on-amazon |date=28 November 2020 }}</ref>

==Usage in distribution networks==
Transporting goods via freight rail networks and container ships is often the most efficient and cost-effective manner of shipping. However, when goods arrive at a high-capacity [[goods station|freight station]] or port, they must then be transported to their final destination. This last leg of the supply chain is often less efficient, comprising up to 53% of the total cost to move goods.<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.businessinsider.com/last-mile-delivery-shipping-explained?IR=T|title=The challenges of last mile delivery logistics and the tech solutions cutting costs in the final mile|last=|first=|date=|website=BUSINESS INSIDER|archive-url=|archive-date=|access-date=}}</ref> This has become known as the "last mile problem".<ref name="udel">{{cite web|last=Scott|first=Martia|title=Improving Freight Movement in Delaware Central Business Districts|url=http://www.ipa.udel.edu/publications/FreightMovementCDBs.pdf|publisher=Institute for Public Administration, University of Delaware|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=November 2009}}</ref><ref>{{cite book|last=Rodrigue|first=Jean-Paul|title=The Geography of Transport Systems|year=2009|publisher=Routledge|isbn=978-0-415-48323-0|edition=2nd|author2=Claude Comtois |author3=Brian Slack |page=212|chapter=The "Last Mile" in Freight Distribution}}</ref> The last mile problem can also include the challenge of making deliveries in urban areas. Deliveries to retail stores, restaurants, and other merchants in a central business district often contribute to congestion and safety problems.<ref name="udel" /><ref>Allen, Brigitte (2012) [http://otago.ourarchive.ac.nz/handle/10523/2247 Improving freight efficiency within the 'last mile': A case study of Wellington's Central Business District] (Thesis, Master of Planning). University of Otago.</ref>

A related last mile problem is the transportation of goods to areas in need of humanitarian relief. Aid supplies are sometimes able to reach a central transportation hub in an affected area but cannot be distributed due to damage caused by a natural disaster or a lack of infrastructure.<ref>{{cite journal|last=Balcik|first=Burcu|author2=Benita M. Beamon|author3=Karen Smilowitz|author3-link=Karen Smilowitz|title=Last Mile Distribution in Humanitarian Relief|journal=Journal of Intelligent Transportation Systems|year=2009|volume=12|issue=2|pages=51–63|url=https://ozyegin.academia.edu/BurcuBalcik/Papers/322500/Last_Mile_Distribution_In_Humanitarian_Relief|accessdate=24 October 2011|issn=1547-2442|doi=10.1080/15472450802023329|s2cid=6063514|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120425091044/http://ozyegin.academia.edu/BurcuBalcik/Papers/322500/Last_Mile_Distribution_In_Humanitarian_Relief|archive-date=25 April 2012|url-status=dead}}</ref>

[[Third-party logistics]] providers have begun to use solutions powered by AI when dealing with last mile. AI tools have improved package tracking information for consumers and retailers. More advanced carrier networks integrated with AI allow third-party logistics providers to offer more shipping options to their customers while also offering preferential rates.<ref>{{Cite web | url=https://synkka.ai/insights/ecommerce-history-and-future-how-ai-is-the-next-key-growth-lever | title=eCommerce History and Future: How AI is The Next Key Growth Lever|last=Maaherra|first=Torbjörn|website=Synkka.ai}}</ref>

==Usage in transportation networks==
[[File:Hiriko 1.jpg|thumb|The [[Hiriko]] folding two-seat [[urban car|urban]] [[electric car]] was intended to be deployed in Germany in 2013 to provide the last mile of the journey to [[Deutsche Bahn]]'s railway customers to their final destinations.<ref name=":0">{{cite web |url=http://green.autoblog.com/2012/12/21/hiriko-folding-ev-will-be-produced-for-german-car-sharing-proj/ |title=Hiriko 'folding' EV will be produced for German car-sharing project next year |author=Danny King |publisher=[[Autoblog Green]] |date=21 December 2012 |accessdate=21 December 2012 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130101084019/http://green.autoblog.com/2012/12/21/hiriko-folding-ev-will-be-produced-for-german-car-sharing-proj/ |archive-date=1 January 2013 |url-status=dead}}</ref>]]

"Last mile" also describes the difficulty in getting people from a [[transportation hub]], especially railway stations, bus depots, and ferry berths, to their final destination. When users have difficulty getting from their starting location to a transportation network, the scenario may alternatively be known as the "first-mile problem".<ref name="mts">{{cite web|title=Using Bicycles for the First and Last Mile of a Commute|url=http://transweb.sjsu.edu/MTIportal/research/publications/documents/BikeCommute.pdf|publisher=Mineta Transportation Institute|date=September 2009|accessdate=24 October 2011}}</ref> In the United States, land-use patterns have moved jobs and people to lower-density suburbs that are often not within [[Walking distance measure|walking distance]] of existing [[public transportation]] options. Therefore, transit use in these areas is often less practical. Critics claim this promotes a [[automobile dependency|reliance on cars]], which results in more traffic congestion, pollution, and [[urban sprawl]].<ref>{{cite web|title=First Steps toward livable communities|url=http://fastlane.dot.gov/2009/03/first-steps-toward-livable-communities.html|work=Fast Lane|publisher=U.S. Department of Transportation|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=22 March 2009|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111015092910/http://fastlane.dot.gov/2009/03/first-steps-toward-livable-communities.html|archive-date=15 October 2011|url-status=dead}}</ref>

Solutions to the last mile problem in public transit have included the use of feeder buses, bicycling infrastructure, and [[urban planning]] reform.<ref>{{cite web|title=FHWA grant funds East Coast's largest bike center; DC transport hub may crack the "last mile" problem|url=http://fastlane.dot.gov/2009/10/fhwa-grant-funds-nations-largest-bike-center-dc-transport-hub-may-crack-the-last-mile-problem.html|work=Fast Lane|publisher=U.S. Department of Transportation|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=5 October 2009|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111015103843/http://fastlane.dot.gov/2009/10/fhwa-grant-funds-nations-largest-bike-center-dc-transport-hub-may-crack-the-last-mile-problem.html|archive-date=15 October 2011|url-status=dead}}</ref> Other methods of alleviating the last mile problem such as [[bicycle sharing system]]s,<ref name="mts" /> [[car sharing]] programs,<ref>{{cite web|last=Kuang|first=Cliff|title=Convenience Is King|url=http://www.good.is/post/convenience-is-king/|work=GOOD Magazine|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=16 April 2009|archive-date=6 July 2012|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120706073255/http://www.good.is/post/convenience-is-king|url-status=dead}}</ref> pod cars ([[personal rapid transit]]),<ref>{{cite web|last=Zax|first=David|title=Can Driverless Pod Cars Solve the 'Last-Mile Problem'?|url=https://www.technologyreview.com/blog/helloworld/27069/|work=Technology Review|publisher=Massachusetts Institute of Technology|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=17 August 2011}}</ref> [[urban air mobility]],<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Moradi |first1=Nima |last2=Wang |first2=Chun |last3=Mafakheri |first3=Fereshteh |title=Urban Air Mobility for Last-Mile Transportation: A Review |journal=Vehicles |date=2024 |volume=6 |issue=3 |pages=1383–1414 |doi=10.3390/vehicles6030066 |doi-access=free}}</ref> and motorized shoes<ref>{{cite web|last=Yvkoff|first=Liane|title=Are motorized shoes the last-mile transport answer?|url=http://reviews.cnet.com/8301-13746_7-20010607-48.html|work=CNet|accessdate=24 October 2011|date=15 July 2010|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120822060159/http://reviews.cnet.com/8301-13746_7-20010607-48.html|archive-date=22 August 2012|url-status=dead}}</ref> have been proposed with varying degrees of adoption. Late in 2015, the [[Ford Motor Company]] received a patent for a "[[self-balancing unicycle|self-propelled unicycle]] engageable with vehicle", which is intended as a last mile commuter solution.<ref>{{cite news|last1=Read|first1=Richard|title=Ford Patent Could Transform Your Car Into A Unicycle|url=http://www.thecarconnection.com/news/1101614_ford-patent-could-transform-your-car-into-a-unicycle|accessdate=10 September 2016|work=The Car Connection|publisher=Internet Brns Automotive Group|date=December 29, 2015}}</ref> Bicycle sharing programs have been successfully implemented in Europe and Asia, and are beginning to be implemented in North America.<ref>{{cite journal|last=DeMaio|first=Paul|title=Bike-sharing: History, Impacts, Models of Provision, and Future|journal=Journal of Public Transportation|year=2009|volume=12|issue=4|pages=41–56|doi=10.5038/2375-0901.12.4.3|doi-access=free}}</ref><ref>{{cite journal|last=Shaheen |first=Susan |author2=Guzman, S., and H. Zhang |title=Bikesharing in Europe, the Americas, and Asia: Past, Present, and Future |journal=Transportation Research Record: Journal of the Transportation Research Board |year=2010 |doi=10.3141/2143-20 |s2cid=40770008 |url=http://76.12.4.249/artman2/uploads/1/Bikesharing_in_Europe__the_Americas__and_Asia.pdf |url-status=dead |archiveurl=https://web.archive.org/web/20120610155608/http://76.12.4.249/artman2/uploads/1/Bikesharing_in_Europe__the_Americas__and_Asia.pdf |archivedate=10 June 2012 }}</ref><ref>{{cite journal|last=Shaheen|first=Susan|author2=Stacey Guzman|title=Worldwide Bikesharing|journal=Access Magazine|year=2011|url=http://uctc.net/access/39/access39_bikesharing.shtml|url-status=dead|archiveurl=https://web.archive.org/web/20120326063609/http://www.uctc.net/access/39/access39_bikesharing.shtml|archivedate=26 March 2012|df=dmy-all}}</ref> Starting in late 2017, micro-mobility services that provide shared vehicles such as dockless [[Scooter-sharing system|electric kick scooters]]<ref>{{Cite news|url=https://www.npr.org/2018/08/29/643058414/dockless-scooters-gain-popularity-and-scorn-across-the-u-s|title=Dockless Scooters Gain Popularity And Scorn Across The U.S.|last=Raphelson|first=Samantha|date=2018-08-29|work=NPR.org|access-date=2018-09-16|language=en}}</ref> or electric-assist bikes<ref>{{Cite news|url=https://chi.streetsblog.org/2018/09/11/jumps-cheaper-dockless-electric-rides-seem-to-be-winning-over-far-south-siders/|title=Jump's Cheaper, Dockless Electric Rides Seem to Be Winning Over Far-South-Siders|last=Greenfield|first=John|date=2018-09-11|work=Streetsblog Chicago|access-date=2018-09-16|language=en-US}}</ref> entered the marketplace. [[Dual-mode vehicle]]s, which can operate on infrastructure and outside of infrastructure, are also considered as a solution to the first mile and last mile problem.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Folsom |first1=Tyler C. |title=Energy and Autonomous Urban Land Vehicles |journal=IEEE Technology and Society Magazine |date=2012 |volume=31 |issue=2 |pages=28–38 |doi=10.1109/MTS.2012.2196339 |bibcode=2012ITSMg..31b..28F |s2cid=6557114 |issn=1937-416X}}</ref> The same dual-mode vehicle can make the journey to a station and from the station on using infrastructure.

== Solutions ==
According to Dutch transport planner, [[Karel Martens (transport planner)|Karel Martens]], bicycles can address the first-mile and last mile problem by increasing the practical distance from which passengers can reach a stop, station from a point of origin or destination from a stop and destination. In the three-country data reviewed by, most bicycle access trips were between 2 and 5 kilometres. Most cyclists using metro stations travelled no farther than 2 kilometres, while about two-thirds of cyclists accessing trains travelled more than 2 kilometres.<ref name=":2">{{Cite journal |last=Martens |first=Karel |date=July 2004 |title=The bicycle as a feedering mode: experiences from three European countries |url=https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1361920904000100 |journal=Transportation Research Part D: Transport and Environment |language=en-US |volume=9 |issue=4 |pages=281–294 |doi=10.1016/j.trd.2004.02.005 |issn=1361-9209 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240419094150/https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1361920904000100 |archive-date=19 April 2024 |access-date=26 September 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>

==Last mile technology platforms==
Due in part to demand on [[Retail|retailers]] and product manufacturers to provide expedited (same and next day) deliveries, tech-enabled last mile technology platforms have emerged. Technological advances have fundamentally transformed how companies manage the final stage of delivery. The use of digital tools and optimized processes not only enhances operational efficiency but also significantly improves customer experience.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Pereira |first=Helene |date=2025-11-05 |title=Last-mile delivery: optimizing the supply chain {{!}} KAIZEN™ |url=https://kaizen.com/insights/last-mile-optimizing-supply-chain/ |access-date=2026-09-14 |language=en-US}}</ref>

Real-time tracking has become a critical competitive advantage. Last-mile delivery tracking systems provide continuous monitoring of order flow, enabling end-to-end visibility across the process.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Pereira |first=Helene |date=2025-11-05 |title=Last-mile delivery: optimizing the supply chain {{!}} KAIZEN™ |url=https://kaizen.com/insights/last-mile-optimizing-supply-chain/ |access-date=2026-09-14 |language=en-US}}</ref> Autonomous technologies transform LMD by reducing human intervention and enhancing operation efficiency, thereby offering scalable solutions across varied environments. These range from UAV or drones, and Autonomous Ground Vehicles (AGVs) to the very important delivery robots, each performing a key role in the reshaping of how deliveries are to be performed, especially in both urban and rural contexts.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Sani |first1=Shuaibu, Abdullahi |last2=Sharif |first2=Mahmoud, Ashraf |last3=Rahil |first3=Sheltami, Tarek |date=March 2025 |title=A Review of Last-Mile Delivery Optimization: Strategies, Technologies, Drone Integration, and Future Trends |url=https://www.mdpi.com/2504-446X/9/3/158 |journal=Drones |language=en |volume=9 |issue=3 |doi=10.3390/drone |doi-broken-date=18 September 2026 |doi-access=free |issn=2504-446X |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260102064611/https://www.mdpi.com/2504-446X/9/3/158 |archive-date=2 January 2026 |access-date=14 September 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref>

[[Delivery drones]], have been important in solving urban congestion and helping raise the speed of delivery. Drones are very viable for small and lightweight packages since they can avoid traffic by flying directly to a delivery point.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Eskandaripour |first1=Hossein |last2=Boldsaikhan |first2=Enkhsaikhan |date=2023-01-21 |title=Last-Mile Drone Delivery: Past, Present, and Future |journal=Drones |language=en |volume=7 |issue=2 |pages=77 |doi=10.3390/drones7020077  |doi-access=free|bibcode=2023Drone...7...77E |issn=2504-446X}}</ref> [[Delivery robot|Delivery robots]] have been increasingly deployed in recent times for last-mile urban deliveries. This class of vehicles will be largely utilized to make short-distance deliveries through heavy traffic, where the human-driven vehicles go through constant delays. According to Ostermeier et al.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Ostermeier |first1=Manuel |last2=Heimfarth |first2=Andreas |last3=Hübner |first3=Alexander |date=October 2023 |title=The multi-vehicle truck-and-robot routing problem for last-mile delivery |url=https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0377221723002552 |journal=European Journal of Operational Research |language=en-US |volume=310 |issue=2 |pages=680–697 |doi=10.1016/j.ejor.2023.03.031 |issn=0377-2217 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240305163202/https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0377221723002552?via%3Dihub |archive-date=5 March 2024 |access-date=14 September 2026 |url-status=live}}</ref> The truck-and-robot systems have a very promising potential for decreasing logistics costs and urban traffic.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Ostermeier |first1=Manuel |last2=Heimfarth |first2=Andreas |last3=Hübner |first3=Alexander |date=October 2023 |title=The multi-vehicle truck-and-robot routing problem for last-mile delivery |url=https://linkinghub.elsevier.com/retrieve/pii/S0377221723002552 |journal=European Journal of Operational Research |language=en |volume=310 |issue=2 |pages=680–697 |doi=10.1016/j.ejor.2023.03.031}}</ref> China’s biggest e-commerce player [[Alibaba Group|Alibaba Group Holding]] unveiled an autonomous delivery robot aimed at bringing down the cost of last-mile deliveries and in line with a broader push towards automated deliveries by e-commerce companies amid the pandemic. Xiaomanlv, which translates into English roughly as ‘little competent donkey’, delivered as many as 500 packages a day to one designated community or campus carrying 50 packages at a time. 

These technologies powered by optimization algorithms plays a key role in reducing mileage, fuel consumption, and cycle times.
